### List of Major League Baseball batting champions

- `[0]` In baseball, batting average (AVG) is a measure of a batter's success rate in achieving a hit during an at bat.
- `[1]` In Major League Baseball (MLB), it is calculated by dividing a player's hits by his at bats (AB).
- `[2]` In MLB, a player in each league wins the "batting title" each season for having the highest batting average that year.
- `[3]` The American League (AL) winner is known as the "Rod Carew American League Batting Champion", while the National League (NL) leader is designated the "Tony Gwynn National League Batting Champion".
- `[4]` Under current rules, a player must have 3.1 plate appearances (PA) per team game (for a total of 502 over the current 162-game season) to qualify for the batting title.
- `[5]` However, if a player's lead in AVG is sufficiently large that enough hitless at bats can be added to reach this requirement and the player still would have the highest batting average, he wins the title.
- `[6]` Tony Gwynn, for example, had 159 hits in 451 ABs in 1996 (.353 average) but only 498 PAs.
- `[7]` Gwynn's batting average would have dropped to .349 (159 hits in 455 ABs) with four hitless ABs added to reach the 502 PA requirement, but this would still have been higher than the next-highest eligible player (.344 average), so he was awarded the 1996 NL batting title.

### Dracula (plant)

- `[2]` The name was applied to the orchid because of the blood-red color of several of the species, the strange aspect of the long spurs of the sepals.

- `[2]` With body sizes ranging from 11 - "Leptofoenus" species are larger than nearly all other species in Pteromalidae.
- `[3]` The genus bears a notable resemblance to the wasp families Pelecinidae, Gasteruptiidae, and Stephanidae.

### Megalurus

- `[0]` Megalurus is a genus of passerine bird in the family Locustellidae.
- `[1]` The genus was once placed in the Old World warbler "wastebin" family Sylviidae.
- `[2]` The genus contains six species also known as the typical grassbirds.
- `[3]` The genus is distributed from northern China and Japan, to India in the west, and Australia in the south, with most species being located wholly or partly in the tropics.
- `[4]` The genus is also sometimes considered to include the genus "Bowdleria", which holds the fernbirds of New Zealand.
- `[5]` The most widespread species, the tawny grassbird, ranges from the Philippines to southern New South Wales, whereas the Fly River grassbird is restricted to swampland in the southern part of New Guinea.
- `[6]` The natural habitat of the typical grassbirds is, as the name suggests, wet grasslands, swamps and other marshlands.
- `[7]` Some species exist away from water in tall grasslands, heathlands, and forest clearings.
- `[8]` Some species have adapted to the margins of rice fields and gardens.

### Pachyornis

- `[0]` Pachyornis is an extinct genus of ratites from New Zealand which belonged to the moa family.
- `[1]` Like all ratites it was a member of the order Struthioniformes.
- `[2]` The Struthioniformes are flightless birds with a sternum without a keel.
- `[3]` They also have a distinctive palate.
- `[4]` This genus contains three species, and are part of the Anomalopteryginae or lesser moa subfamily.
- `[5]` "Pachyornis" moa were the stoutest and most heavy-legged genus of the family.
- `[6]` The most notable species being "Pachyornis elephantopus" - the Heavy-Footed Moa.
- `[7]` They were generally similar to the Eastern Moa or the Broad-billed moa of the genus "Euryapteryx", but differed in having a pointed bill and being more heavyset in general.
- `[8]` At least one species ("P. australis") is assumed to have had a crest of long feathers on its head.
- `[9]` The species became rapidly extinct following human colonization of New Zealand, with the possible exception of "P. australis", which may have already been extinct by then.

### Calyptocephalellidae

- `[0]` The Calyptocephalellidae are a family of toads found in Chile containing two genera, "Calyptocephalella" and "Telmatobufo".
- `[1]` The "Calyptocephalella" genus contains one species, "C. gayi", the helmeted water toad, which is a large aquatic toad weighing up to 0.5 kg .
- `[2]` The "Telmatobufo" genus contains four species, "T. australis", "T. bullocki", "T. ignotus", and "T. venustus".
- `[3]` All species within the family are considered threatened, with "T. bullocki" and "T. venustus" being classified as critically endangered.

### Crowea

- `[0]` Crowea is a genus of small evergreen shrubs in the plant family Rutaceae sometimes known as Waxflowers.
- **[SF]** `[1]` There are three species and many subspecies and cultivars, all of which are popular as ornamentals because of their abundant, attractive flowers which often occur during autumn and winter.
- `[2]` "Crowea" species are all endemic to Australia, where they occur in Victoria, New South Wales and Western Australia.

### Lophodermium

- `[0]` Lophodermium is a genus of fungi within the family Rhytismataceae.
- `[1]` The genus contains 145 species and has a global distribution.
- `[2]` Species of this genus are usually observed producing zone lines, conidiomata and ascomata on dead fallen leaves, but at least some are known to colonize living leaves.
- `[3]` In many cases they then live inside the colonized leaf as a symptomless endobiont, where they are regarded as detritivores utilising dead plant matter.
- `[4]` In a few cases they may kill all or part of the leaf prematurely, and there is a substantial literature dealing with those species as plant pathogens.
- `[5]` The genus infects many different plant families but with a notable concentration in the family Pinaceae; many "Lophodermium" species are restricted to a single host genus (or even species), but some, particularly those infecting grasses, may infect several genera.
- `[6]` Some are economically important plant pathogens, such as those that cause needlecast disease in European Black Pine, Scots Pine and Red Pine in forestry and christmas tree plantations.
- `[7]` In these species, notably "L. pinastri" and "L. seditiosum", the fungal spores disperse and infect the pine needles in late summer, which turn brown by the following spring and then fall off.

### Banksiamyces

- `[0]` Banksiamyces is a genus of fungi in the order Helotiales, with a tentative placement in the family Helotiaceae.
- `[1]` The genus contains four species, which grow on the seed follicles of the dead infructescences or "cones" of various species of "Banksia", a genus in the plant family Proteaceae endemic to Australia.
- `[2]` Fruit bodies of the fungus appear as small (typically less than 10 mm diameter), shallow dark cups on the follicles of the "Banksia" fruit.
- `[3]` The edges of dry fruit bodies fold inwards, appearing like narrow slits.
- `[4]` The first specimens of "Banksiamyces", known then as "Tympanis toomansis", were described in 1887.
- `[5]` Specimens continued to be collected occasionally for almost 100 years before becoming examined more critically in the early 1980s, leading to the creation of a new genus to contain what was determined to be three distinct species, "B. katerinae", "B. macrocarpus", and "B. toomansis".
- `[6]` A fourth species, "B. maccannii", was added in 1984.

### Ceratozamia

- `[0]` Ceratozamia is a genus of New World cycads in the family Zamiaceae.
- `[1]` The genus contains 27 known currently living species and one or two fossil species.
- `[2]` Most species are endemic to mountainous areas of Mexico, while few species extend into the mountains of Guatemala, Honduras and Belize.
- `[3]` The genus name comes from the Greek "ceras", meaning horn, which refers to the paired, spreading horny projections on the male and female sporophylls of all species.

### List of Amanita species

- `[0]` The following is a list of some notable species of the agaric genus "Amanita".
- `[1]` This genus contains over 500 named species and varieties, but the list is far from exhaustive.
- `[2]` The list follows the classification of subgenera and sections of "Amanita" outline by Corner and Bas; Bas, as used by Tulloss (2007) and modified by Redhead & al. (2016) for "Amanita" subgenus "Amanitina" and Singer for "Amanita" section "Roanokenses".
- `[3]` Bolding of the species name and an asterisk (*) following indicates the species is the type species of that section, with a double asterisk (**) indicating the type species of the entire genus.
- `[4]` Use of common names follows Tulloss (2007), Holden (2003), Arora (1986), and Lincoff (1981).

## 58. What is the chemical formula of the organic material that clementines have less of than oranges?

**Supporting facts** (title, sentence index):

**Context:**

### Petrified wood

- `[0]` Petrified wood (from the Greek root "petro" meaning "rock" or "stone"; literally "wood turned into stone") is the name given to a special type of fossilized remains of terrestrial vegetation.
- `[1]` It is the result of a tree or tree-like plants having completely transitioned to stone by the process of permineralization.
- `[2]` All the organic materials have been replaced with minerals (mostly a silicate, such as quartz), while retaining the original structure of the stem tissue.
- `[3]` Unlike other types of fossils which are typically impressions or compressions, petrified wood is a three-dimensional representation of the original organic material.
- `[4]` The petrifaction process occurs underground, when wood becomes buried under sediment or volcanic ash and is initially preserved due to a lack of oxygen which inhibits aerobic decomposition.
- `[5]` Mineral-laden water flowing through the covering material deposits minerals in the plant's cells; as the plant's lignin and cellulose decay, a stone mold forms in its place.
- `[6]` The organic matter needs to become petrified before it decomposes completely.
- `[7]` A forest where such material has petrified becomes known as a petrified forest.

### Biointerface

- `[0]` A biointerface is the region of contact between a biomolecule, cell, biological tissue or living organism or organic material considered living with another biomaterial or inorganic/organic material.
- `[1]` The motivation for biointerface science stems from the urgent need to increase the understanding of interactions between biomolecules and surfaces.
- `[2]` The behavior of complex macromolecular systems at materials interfaces are important in the fields of biology, biotechnology, diagnostics, and medicine.
- `[3]` Biointerface science is a multidisciplinary field in which (bio)chemists who synthesize novel classes of biomolecules (PNA, peptidomimetics, aptamers, ribozymes, and engineered proteins) cooperate with scientists who have developed the tools to position biomolecules with molecular precision (proximal probe methods, nano-and micro contact methods, e-beam and X-ray lithography, and bottom up self-assembly methods), scientists who have developed new spectroscopic techniques to interrogate these molecules at the solid-liquid interface, and people who integrate these into functional devices (applied physicists, analytical chemists and bioengineers).

### Citric acid

- **[SF]** `[0]` Citric acid is a weak organic tricarboxylic acid having the chemical formula CHO.
- `[1]` It occurs naturally in citrus fruits.
- `[2]` In biochemistry, it is an intermediate in the citric acid cycle, which occurs in the metabolism of all aerobic organisms.

### Dissolved organic carbon

- `[0]` Dissolved organic carbon (DOC), sometimes known as dissolved organic material (DOM), is a broad classification for organic molecules of varied origin and composition within aquatic systems.
- `[1]` The "dissolved" fraction of organic carbon is an operational classification.
- `[2]` Many researchers use the term "dissolved" for compounds below 0.45 micrometers, but 0.22 micrometers is also common, saving colloidal for higher concentrations.
- `[3]` A practical definition of dissolved typically used in marine chemistry is all substances that pass through a GF/F filter.
- `[4]` The recommended measure technique is the HTCO technique after filtration on precombusted glass fiber filters, typically GF/F filters.

### Clementine

- `[0]` A clementine ("Citrus × clementina") is a hybrid between a mandarin orange and a sweet orange, so named in 1902.
- `[1]` The exterior is a deep orange colour with a smooth, glossy appearance.
- `[2]` Clementines can be separated into 7 to 14 segments.
- `[3]` Similar to tangerines, they tend to be easy to peel.
- `[4]` The clementine is also occasionally referred to as the "Moroccan clementine".
- **[SF]** `[5]` They are typically juicy and sweet, with less acid than oranges.
- `[6]` Their oils, like other citrus fruits, contain mostly limonene as well as myrcene, linalool, α-pinene and many complex aromatics.

### Organic matter

- `[0]` Organic matter, organic material, or natural organic matter (NOM) refers to the large pool of carbon-based compounds found within natural and engineered, terrestrial and aquatic environments.
- `[1]` It is matter composed of organic compounds that has come from the remains of organisms such as plants and animals and their waste products in the environment.
- `[2]` Organic molecules can also be made by chemical reactions that don't involve life.
- `[3]` Basic structures are created from cellulose, tannin, cutin, and lignin, along with other various proteins, lipids, and carbohydrates.
- `[4]` Organic matter is very important in the movement of nutrients in the environment and plays a role in water retention on the surface of the planet.

### Structural formula

- `[0]` The structural formula of a chemical compound is a graphic representation of the molecular structure, showing how the atoms are arranged.
- `[1]` The chemical bonding within the molecule is also shown, either explicitly or implicitly.
- `[2]` Unlike chemical formulas, which have a limited number of symbols and are capable of only limited descriptive power, structural formulas provide a complete geometric representation of the molecular structure.
- `[3]` For example, many chemical compounds exist in different isomeric forms, which have different enantiomeric structures but the same chemical formula.
- `[4]` A structural formula is able to indicate arrangements of atoms in three dimensional space in a way that a chemical formula may not be able to do.

### Postpipe

- `[0]` In archaeology, a postpipe (or post pipe) is the remains of an upright timber placed in a posthole.
- `[1]` Given the right conditions, timbers may survive over long periods of time and a recovered postpipe can simply be of solid wood.
- `[2]` Under less preservative conditions however, only a dark circular stain of organic material may be left in the fill of the posthole observable in plan and section.
- `[3]` This differs in consistency from the less organic backfill of the posthole and can be identified simply through this change in make-up.
- `[4]` The size and depth and of the postpipe can provide information as to any reuse of the posthole especially if several different postpipes can be identified.
- `[5]` They can also indicate the species of wood used and help suggest the nature of the structure that the timber once supported.
- `[6]` The term was first used by Maud Cunnington when she excavated the site of Woodhenge in the English county of Wiltshire during the 1920s and found numerous examples of decomposed timber posts.

### Detritus

- `[0]` In biology, detritus ( ) is dead particulate organic material (as opposed to dissolved organic material).
- `[1]` It typically includes the bodies or fragments of dead organisms as well as fecal material.
- `[2]` Detritus is typically colonized by communities of microorganisms which act to decompose (or remineralize) the material.
- `[3]` In terrestrial ecosystems, it is encountered as leaf litter and other organic matter intermixed with soil, which is referred to as humus.
- `[4]` Detritus of aquatic ecosystems is organic material suspended in water and piling up on seabed floors, which is referred to as marine snow.

### Campigliaite

- `[0]` Campigliaite is a copper and manganese sulfate mineral with a chemical formula of CuMn(SO)(OH)·4HO.
- `[1]` It has a chemical formula and also a crystal structure similar to niedermayrite, with Cd(II) cation replacing by Mn(II).
- `[2]` The formation of campigliaite is related to the oxidation of sulfide minerals to form sulfate solutions with ilvaite associated with the presence of manganese.
- `[3]` Campigliaite is a rare secondary mineral formed when metallic sulfide skarn deposits are oxidized.
- `[4]` While there are several related associations, there is no abundant source for this mineral due to its rare process of formation.
- `[5]` Based on its crystallographic data and chemical formula, campigliaite is placed in the devillite group and considered the manganese analogue of devillite.
- `[6]` Campigliaite belongs to the copper oxysalt minerals as well followed by the subgroup M=M-T sheets.
- `[7]` The infinite sheet structures that campigliaite has are characterized by strongly bonded polyhedral sheets, which are linked in the third dimension by weaker hydrogen bonds.

- `[1]` The genus contains 145 species and has a global distribution.
- `[2]` Species of this genus are usually observed producing zone lines, conidiomata and ascomata on dead fallen leaves, but at least some are known to colonize living leaves.
- `[3]` In many cases they then live inside the colonized leaf as a symptomless endobiont, where they are regarded as detritivores utilising dead plant matter.
- `[4]` In a few cases they may kill all or part of the leaf prematurely, and there is a substantial literature dealing with those species as plant pathogens.
- `[5]` The genus infects many different plant families but with a notable concentration in the family Pinaceae; many "Lophodermium" species are restricted to a single host genus (or even species), but some, particularly those infecting grasses, may infect several genera.
- `[6]` Some are economically important plant pathogens, such as those that cause needlecast disease in European Black Pine, Scots Pine and Red Pine in forestry and christmas tree plantations.
- `[7]` In these species, notably "L. pinastri" and "L. seditiosum", the fungal spores disperse and infect the pine needles in late summer, which turn brown by the following spring and then fall off.

### Hoffman v. Jones

- `[0]` Hoffman v. Jones, (Fla. 1973), was a case decided by the Supreme Court of Florida that was the first adoption of the comparative negligence rule in negligence law through judicial decision as opposed to adoption through statute.
- `[1]` In the wrongful death case of Hoffman v. Jones, attorney Sammy Cacciatore Jr. was instrumental in causing the Florida Supreme Court to adopt for the first time the comparative negligence rule in negligence law.William Harrison Jones Jr. was killed by a Pav-A-Way Corporation truck driven by Philip Francis Hoffman Jr.
- `[2]` It was the first case in the nation in which a state supreme court abandoned the almost-150-year-old doctrine of contributory negligence, which precluded an injured victim from recovery if the victim had contributed to the incident to any degree.
- `[3]` The Florida Supreme Court adopted the concept of “pure” comparative negligence, which allows a victim to be compensated for the percentage of harm caused by the at-fault person.
- `[4]` The decision of the court in Hoffman v. Jones has been cited in law school textbooks, and now the concept of comparative negligence is the prevailing doctrine.

- `[1]` The case arose out of the issuance of Executive Order 9066 following the December 1941 attack on Pearl Harbor and the U.S. entry into World War II.

Banaitis, was a case decided before the Supreme Court of the United States, dealing with the issue of whether the portion of a money judgment or settlement paid to a taxpayer's attorney under a contingent-fee agreement is income to the taxpayer for federal income tax purposes.
- `[1]` The Supreme Court held when a taxpayer's recovery constitutes income, the taxpayer's income includes the portion of the recovery paid to the attorney as a contingent fee.
- `[2]` Employment cases are an exception to this Supreme Court ruling because of the Civil Rights Tax Relief in the American Jobs Creation Act of 2004.

Arizona" and is aware he or she has the right to remain silent, but does not explicitly invoke or waive the right.

### United States v. Quiver

- `[0]` United States v. Quiver, 241 U.S. 602 (1916) is a case decided by the United States Supreme Court after first appearing in United States District Court for the District of South Dakota.
- `[1]` The case argued on February 28, 1916 and decided on June 12, 1916 concerned adultery committed on the Pine Ridge Indian Reservation in South Dakota between two enrolled members of the Oglala Sioux Tribe.
- `[2]` The district court had held that adultery committed by an Indian with another Indian on an Indian reservation was not punishable under the act of March 3, 1887, c. 397, 24 Stat.
- `[3]` 635, now § 316 of the Penal Code.
- `[4]` This decision was made because the offense occurred on a Sioux Indian reservation which is not said to be under jurisdiction of the district court.
- `[5]` The United States Supreme Court affirmed the judgment of the district court saying that the adultery was not punishable as it had occurred between two American Indians on an American Indian reservation.

### V.L. v. E.L.

- `[0]` V.L. v. E.L., 577 U.S. ___ (2016) , is a case decided by the Supreme Court of the United States concerning the adoption rights of same-sex couples.
- `[1]` In 2007, a Georgia Superior Court granted adoption rights to V.L., the partner of E.L., the woman who gave birth to their three children.
- `[2]` However, after moving back to Alabama, the couple split up.
- `[3]` E.L. tried to block V.L. from seeing the children, but V.L. filed a lawsuit seeking visitation and other parental rights.
- `[4]` On September 18, 2015, the Supreme Court of Alabama ruled that the state did not have to recognize the adoption judgment, saying that the Georgia court misapplied its own state law.
- `[5]` The court voided the recognition of the adoption judgment in Alabama.
- `[6]` V.L. petitioned the United States Supreme Court to stay the ruling during her appeal and allow her to see her children.
- `[7]` On December 14, 2015, the Supreme Court stayed the ruling pending their action on a petition for a writ of certiorari filed by V.L. On March 7, 2016, the Supreme Court of the United States reversed the decision of the Alabama Supreme Court by per curiam summary disposition.

### Talkback (recording)

- `[0]` In sound recording, talkback refers to the intercom system used in recording studios and production control rooms (PCR) in television studios to enable personnel to communicate with people in the recording area or booth.
- `[1]` While the control room can hear the person in the booth over the studio microphones, the person in the booth hears the control room over a PA, monitor speaker, in their headphones or Interruptible feedback (IFB) earpiece.
- `[2]` Take numbers, reference data, and sometimes count-ins or remarks are also "stamped" onto recordings through talkback, similar to a clapperboard.

### Cargo control room

- `[0]` The cargo control room, CCR, or cargo office of a tankship is where the person in charge (PIC) can monitor and control the loading and unloading of the ship's liquid cargo.
- `[1]` Prevalent on automated vessels, the CCR may be in its own room, or located on the ship's bridge.
- `[2]` Among other things, the equipment in the CCR may allow the person in charge to control cargo and stripping pumps, control and monitor valve positions, and monitor cargo tank liquid levels.

### Control room

- `[0]` A control room, operations center, or operations control center (OCC) is a room serving as a central space where a large physical facility or physically dispersed service can be monitored and controlled.
- `[1]` A control room will often be part of a larger command center.

### Production control

- `[0]` Production control is the activity of monitoring and controlling any particular production or operation.
- `[1]` Production control is often run from a specific control room or operations room.
- `[2]` With inventory control and quality control, production control is one of the key functions of operations management.

### Master control

- `[0]` Master control is the technical hub of a broadcast operation common among most over-the-air television stations and television networks.
- `[1]` It is distinct from a production control room (PCR) in television studios where the activities such as switching from camera to camera are coordinated.
- `[2]` It is also vastly different from the studio where the talent are located.
- `[3]` A transmission control room (TCR) is usually smaller in size and is a scaled down version of centralcasting.

### Distributed control system

- `[0]` A distributed control system (DCS) is a computerised control system for a process or plant usually with a large number of control loops, in which autonomous controllers are distributed throughout the system, but there is central operator supervisory control.
- `[1]` This is in contrast to non-distributed control systems that use centralised controllers; either discrete controllers located at a central control room or within a central computer.
- `[2]` The DCS concept increases reliability and reduces installation costs by localising control functions near the process plant, with remote monitoring and supervision.

### Production control room

- `[0]` The production control room or studio control room (SCR) is the place in a television studio in which the composition of the outgoing program takes place.
- `[1]` An SCR is also often the acronym for the Satellite Control Room, from here TV feeds are sent to & received from the local Satellite used by the TV station

### Transmission control room

- `[0]` Transmission control room (TCR) or ' transmission suite' , or ' Tx room' , or "presentation" is a room at broadcast facilities and television stations around the world.
- `[1]` Compared to a Master Control Room, it is usually smaller in size and is a scaled-down version of centralcasting.
- `[2]` The TX Room or Presentation suite are staffed 24x7 by Presentation Coordinators and Tape Operators and is fitted out with video play out systems often using server based broadcast automation.

### Meeting Resistance

- **[SF]** `[0]` Meeting Resistance is a 2007 documentary film about the Iraq War.
- `[1]` The film presents the views of eleven Iraqi resistance fighters in the Adhamiyah neighborhood of Baghdad.
- `[2]` The film was directed by journalists Molly Bingham (United States) and Steve Connors (UK).
